Normal Form.
We generalize the normal form of a consistency model to take into account conflict predicates. A consistency model in normal form only contains visibility formulas that are simple, non-vacuous and “conflict-maximal”.
A
conflict-strengthening
of a visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
is a visibility formula
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathsf{v}
by (1) replacing some occurrence of
\mathsf{conflict}(E)
(resp.,
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E)
) with
\mathsf{conflict}(E^{\prime})
(resp.,
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E^{\prime})
) where
E^{\prime}
is a strict superset of
E
or (2) removing predicate
\mathsf{conflict}(E)
if
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E)
also belongs to
\mathsf{v}
. A visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
is
conflict-maximal
w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
iff there is no conflict-strengthening
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
such that for every execution
\xi
over events in
\mathsf{Events}[{\mathsf{OpSpec}}]
, object
x
, and events
e_{0},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}
, if
\mathsf{v}_{x}(e_{0},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})})
holds in
\xi
, then
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}_{x}(e_{0},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})})
holds in
\xi
as well. A consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
is
conflict-maximal
w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
iff all its visibility formulas are conflict-maximal w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
.

For example, if
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, any instance of
\varepsilon_{i}
must write on some object
y
. In conflict-maximal visibility formulas, this fact is represented with a conflict predicate (
\mathsf{conflict}(E)
or
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E)
) s.t.
\varepsilon_{i-1}\in E
. If
\mathsf{OpSpec}
requires that every event reading
y
also writes on
y
, then in a conflict-maximal visibility formula, both
\varepsilon_{i-1},\varepsilon_{i}
belong to
E
. In general, if in any abstract execution, the events instantiating
\varepsilon_{i_{1}},\ldots,\varepsilon_{i_{j}}
from
\mathsf{v}_{x}
always conflict (resp. they always write
x
), then the visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
must contain the predicate
\mathsf{conflict}(\varepsilon_{i_{1}},\ldots,\varepsilon_{i_{j}})
(resp.
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\varepsilon_{i_{1}},\ldots,\varepsilon_{i_{j}})
).

Definition 7.1.

A consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
is called in
normal form w.r.t. a operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
if it contains only simple, conflict-maximal visibility formulas and no visibility formula from
\mathsf{CMod}
is vacuous w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
.

Under some operation specifications, consistency models can be equivalent due to conflict predicates. For example, in a storage with only
\mathtt{FAA}
operations,
SI
and
SER
are equivalent due to the
Conflict
visibility formula: in this specification, every event is both a read and a write event and so any event reading
x
conflicts with an event writing
x
.

Similarly to
Section

6
, we say that a consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
is
arbitration-free
w.r.t. an operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
if there exists a consistency model in general normal form w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
that is equivalent to
\mathsf{CMod}
and whose visibility formulas are arbitration-free.
Appendix

B
demonstrates the existence of a normal form and shows that it is not possible for two normal forms to differ solely in that one includes only arbitration-free visibility formulas while the other does not. This result confirms that arbitration-freedom is not a property of the chosen normal form, but rather an inherent characteristic of the definitions of
\mathsf{CMod}
and
\mathsf{OpSpec}
.

Causal Suffix Closure.

We introduce an assumption about consistency models which is used in the proof of the AFC theorem in order to find counterexamples to availability that involve only two replicas. This assumption is satisfied by all practical cases that we are aware of (see
Example

7.3
).

Therefore, we assume that every normal form
\mathsf{CMod}
of a consistency model is
closed under causal suffixes
, i.e., for every visibility formula
\mathsf{v}_{x}\in\mathsf{CMod}
,
\mathsf{CMod}
contains every arbitration-free “suffix” of
\mathsf{v}_{x}
that starts with an event writing
x
. Thinking about a visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
as a path of dependencies (between the pairs
(\varepsilon_{i-1},\varepsilon_{i})
), a suffix of
\mathsf{v}
is a suffix of that path. For example, the visibility formulas
s
and
s^{\prime}
described in
Equation

9
and
Equation

10
are suffixes of the visibility formula in
Equation

8
.

(8)

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{x}(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{3})=\exists\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}.(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}\land(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}\land(\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{3})\neq\emptyset\land\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})

(9)

\displaystyle s_{x}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{3})=\exists\varepsilon_{2}.(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}\ \land\ (\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\ \land\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{3})\neq\emptyset\land\ \mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})

(10)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{x}(\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3})=(\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\ \land\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{3})\neq\emptyset\land\ \mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\varepsilon_{2})

Formally, let
\mathsf{v}_{x}
be a visibility formula defined as in
Equation

7
. Let
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\mathsf{v}_{x})
be the union of the sets
E
such that
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E)
occurs in the definition of
\mathsf{v}_{x}
. For any variable
\varepsilon_{k}\in\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\mathsf{v}_{x})
, the
\varepsilon_{k}
-suffix of
\mathsf{v}_{x}
is the formula obtained by (1) removing the quantifiers for the first
k
quantified events,
e_{1}\ldots e_{k}
, and (2) removing all occurrences of the (now) free variables
e_{0},\ldots e_{k-1}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathsf{suff}_{x}(\mathsf{v}_{x},k)(\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{n})\Coloneqq\exists\varepsilon_{k+1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n-1}.\bigwedge_{i=k+1}^{n}(\varepsilon_{i-1},\varepsilon_{i})\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}_{i}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(\varepsilon_{n})\neq\emptyset\land\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(\varepsilon_{k},\ldots\varepsilon_{n})

where

\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(\varepsilon_{k},\ldots\varepsilon_{n})
is obtained from
\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(\varepsilon_{0},\ldots\varepsilon_{n})
by projecting all the conflict predicates over the set of events
E_{k}=\{\varepsilon_{k},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\}
, i.e., a predicate
\mathsf{conflict}(E)
(resp.
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E)
) occurs in
\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(\varepsilon_{0},\ldots\varepsilon_{n})
iff
\mathsf{conflict}(E\cap E_{k})
(resp.
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E\cap E_{k})
) occurs in
\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(\varepsilon_{k},\ldots\varepsilon_{n})
.

We refer to arbitration-free suffixes as
causal
, since the remaining dependencies intuitively reflect broader notions of causality.
The intuition behind this notion of closure is that the context of an invocation should be upward-closed with respect to causality—meaning that if an update (writing
x
) is included, then any later updates (writing
x
) along the dependency path defined by the visibility formula that lie in its causal past must also be included.

We say that a visibility formula
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}

subsumes
a visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
of the same length if for every
i,1\leq i\leq\mathsf{len}(v)
,
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}
is stronger or equal than
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}
. We say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
is stronger than
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
is stronger than
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
. The extension of “being stronger” to any relation
\mathsf{Rel}
described using
Equation

3
is done as expected, as all our operators are positive (there are no negations).

Definition 7.2.

A consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
is
closed under causal suffixes
if for every
\mathsf{v}_{x}\in\mathsf{CMod}
and
\varepsilon_{k}\in\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\mathsf{v}_{x})
,
\mathsf{CMod}
includes some visibility formula
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}
that subsumes every arbitration-free suffix of
v
.

Example 7.3.

A consistency model containing the visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
in
Equation

8
must also contain the visibility formula
s^{\prime}
in order to be closed under causal suffixes. Note that
s
uses arbitration and it is not required to be included.

Any basic consistency model is closed under causal suffixes because every basic visibility formula has no proper arbitration-free suffix. Indeed,
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\mathsf{v}_{x})
contains just the first event
\varepsilon_{0}
(assuming that
{\varepsilon_{0}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
is rewritten as
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(\{\varepsilon_{0}\})
).
The models described in
Figures

4
and
7
are trivially closed under causal suffixes because their visibility formulas have no arbitration-free suffixes.

7.2.
Operation Specifications

We generalize operation specifications to allow operations to access (read or write) multiple objects, and to support read values that are not limited to the inputs of individual write operations. For example, this includes multi-value reads that return all concurrently written values for an object, or counter reads that return an aggregated value computed from all observed increments.

The generalized reading behavior is modeled using two functions
\mathsf{rspec}
and
\mathsf{extract}
described hereafter. We also introduce a generalized
\mathsf{wspec}
function. Therefore,
\mathsf{rspec}
selects from a given context the events (updates) which are relevant for a reading invocation,
\mathsf{extract}
defines the value read by an invocation, if any (based on the output of
\mathsf{rspec}
), and
\mathsf{wspec}
defines the value written by an invocation, if any (to model conditional read-writes, this is based on the output of
\mathsf{extract}
).

Definition 7.4.

A
read specification

\mathsf{rspec}:\mathsf{Events}\to\mathsf{Objs}\to\mathsf{Contexts}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Events})
is a function such that for every object
x
, context
c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}
and event
e
:

(1)

well-formedness:
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)\subseteq E
, and if
e
is an initial event,
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)=\emptyset
, and

(2)

unconditional reading: if
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)\neq\emptyset
for some context
c
, then for every non-empty context
c^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c^{\prime})\neq\emptyset

Equations

11
,
12
and
13
describe the read specifications of
\mathsf{faacas}
, a key-value store
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
with
\mathtt{PUT}(x,v)
and multi-value
\mathtt{GET}(x)
operations (
Section

A.2
),
and a collection of distributed counters
\mathsf{counter}
with
\mathtt{inc}(x)
and
\mathtt{rd}(x)
operations (
Section

A.3
). Concerning the relationship to
basic
read specifications, note that the
\mathsf{faacas}
specification in
Equation

4
was simpler because the constraint from
Equation

11
was imposed in the notion of validity for abstraction executions (
Definition

4.2
). For multi-value reads (
Equation

12
), the read specification selects the maximal elements in the receive-before relation (which models causality), and for a counter (
Equation

13
), it returns all events in the context.

(11)

{\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}}E\},&\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{GET}(x),\mathtt{FAA}(x,v),\mathtt{CAS}(x,v,v^{\prime})\}\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}\\
\ \ \emptyset,&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.}

(12)

{\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}}E,&\text{if }r=\mathtt{GET}(x)\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}\\
\ \ \emptyset,&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.}

(13)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}E,&\text{if }r=\mathtt{rd}(x)\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}\\
\ \ \emptyset,&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The extract specification below computes the value returned from an object
x
based on the set of invocations writing
x
returned by the read specification which are paired with values they write (this will become clearer when defining the application of these functions on an abstract execution).

Definition 7.5.

An
extract specification

\mathsf{extract}:\mathsf{Events}\to\mathsf{Objs}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Events}\times\mathsf{Vals})\to\mathsf{Vals}
, such that
\mathsf{extract}(\textup{{{init}}})
is defined for every initial event
init
.

Equation

14
describes the extract specification of
\mathsf{faacas}
: the value extracted for
\mathtt{GET}
,
\mathtt{FAA}
and
\mathtt{CAS}
coincides with the value written by some previous
\mathtt{PUT}
/
\mathtt{FAA}
/
\mathtt{CAS}
operation.
Equation

15
describes the extract specification of
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
: the value extracted for
\mathtt{GET}
is the set of values written by some previous
\mathtt{PUT}
. In the case of
\mathsf{counter}
,
Equation

16
, the value extracted for
\mathtt{rd}
returns the number of increment invocations in the input, which equals
|R|
minus one for the initial event
init
which is always included in
R
(since it is
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
before all other events).

(14)

{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{GET}(x),\mathtt{FAA}(x,v^{\prime}),\mathtt{CAS}(x,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\}\text{ and }R=\{(w,v)\}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\end{array}}

(15)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{v\ |\ (\_,v)\in R\}&\text{if }r=\mathtt{GET}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\end{array}

(16)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|R|-1&\text{if }r=\mathtt{rd}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Finally, the write specification computes the value written by an invocation to an object
x
, based on the values it reads. This makes it possible to model atomic read-writes, e.g., a compare-and-swap, which may write or not depending on what they read, or the value they write may change depending on what they read, e.g., a Fetch-and-Add.

Definition 7.6.

A
write specification

\mathsf{wspec}:\mathsf{Events}\to\mathsf{Objs}\to\mathsf{Vals}\to\mathsf{Vals}
is a function such that
\mathsf{wspec}(\textup{{{init}}})
is defined for every initial event
init
.

The write specification of
\mathsf{faacas}
and
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
,
Equation

17
, describes that its write operations are
\mathtt{PUT}
,
\mathtt{FAA}
and
\mathtt{CAS}
.
\mathtt{PUT}
and
\mathtt{FAA}
unconditionally writes on
x
while
\mathtt{CAS}
does it depending on the read-and-extracted value of
x
; where
x
is the only object accessed by the invocation. In the case of
\mathsf{counter}
,
Equation

19
, only the operation
\mathtt{inc}(x)
writes, writing a dummy value
1
just to indicate that the write has taken place.

(17)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{PUT}(x,v^{\prime})\\
v+v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{FAA}(x,v^{\prime})\\
v^{\prime\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{CAS}(x,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\land v=v^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(18)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{PUT}(x,v^{\prime})\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(19)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,\_)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }w=\mathtt{inc}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Definition 7.7.

An
operation specification
is a tuple
\mathsf{OpSpec}=(E,\mathsf{rspec},\mathsf{extract},\mathsf{wspec})
where
E
is a set of events.
\mathsf{Events}[{\mathsf{OpSpec}}]
refers to the set of events
E
in an operation specification.

Appendix

A
contains more examples of operation specifications, including SQL statements.

7.3.
Validity w.r.t. Storage Specifications

We extend the notion of validity for abstract executions to (general) storage specifications, in a way that is similar to the case of basic storage specifications (
Section

4.3
).

We use the extension of
\mathsf{rspec},\mathsf{extract}
, and
\mathsf{wspec}
to abstract executions defined below:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])=\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(e,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}]))

\displaystyle\mathsf{extract}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])=\mathsf{extract}(e)\left(x,\left\{(e^{\prime},\mathtt{wval}(e)(x))\left|\ e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])\right.\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{wspec}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])=\mathsf{wspec}(e)(x,\mathsf{extract}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}]))

Definition 7.8.

Let
\mathsf{Spec}=(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
be a storage specification. An abstract execution
\xi={({h,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}
of a history
{h}={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
is
valid
w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
iff

•

\xi
contains events from the operation specification, i.e.,
E\subseteq\mathsf{Events}[{\mathsf{OpSpec}}]
,

•

for every event
r\in E
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)=\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])
, and

•

the value written by each event
e\in E
to object
x
is consistent with
\mathsf{wspec}
, i.e.,
\mathtt{wval}(e)(x)=\mathsf{wspec}(e)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])
.

A history
{h}
is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
iff there exists an abstract execution of
{h}
which is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
.

Observe that
Definition

7.8
coincides with
Definition

4.2
for storage systems that also admit basic storage specifications, e.g.,
\mathsf{faacas}
.

7.4.
Assumptions About Operation Specifications

To avoid pathological behaviors in the generalization of specifications, we make several assumptions.

Maximally-Layered Read Specifications.

For any basic operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
, the validity of an abstract execution w.r.t. a stronger consistency model (and
\mathsf{OpSpec}
) implies validity w.r.t. a weaker one (see
Lemma

6.5
). In general, this is not true for operation specifications as described in this section (see
Example

7.9
). Therefore, we introduce an assumption about read specifications, called
maximally-layered
, which ensures that this property remains true.

init

e_{0}

e_{1}

\mathtt{GET}(x)

e_{2}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}

\mathtt{PUT}
and
\mathtt{GET}
.

\mathtt{GET}(x)

.

e_{1}

Example 7.9.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}={({E,\mathsf{rspec},\mathsf{extract},\mathsf{wspec}})}
be an operation specification of a key-value store with
\mathtt{GET}
and
\mathtt{PUT}
operations whose read specification is given by
Equation

20
.

(20)

\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}}E\}&\text{if }\nexists e^{\prime}\in E\text{ s.t. }\mathtt{rep}(e)\neq\mathtt{rep}(e^{\prime})\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}\\
\textup{{{init}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

We compare the validity of the abstract execution
\xi
depicted in
Figure

8
w.r.t.
SC
and
CC
(observe that
\textup{{CC}}\preccurlyeq\textup{{SC}}
). Under
SC
both
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
are visible to
e_{2}
, which implies
\mathsf{rspec}(e_{2})(x,[\xi,\textup{{SC}}])=\{\textup{{{init}}}\}
. Therefore,
\xi
is valid w.r.t.
SC
. However, under
CC
, only
e_{1}
is visible to
e_{2}
, which implies
\mathsf{rspec}(e_{2})(x,[\xi,\textup{{CC}}])=\{e_{1}\}
, and therefore,
\xi
is not valid w.r.t.
CC
.

Let
\leq
be a partial order over a set
A
. A chain of
\leq
is a subset of
A
which is totally ordered w.r.t.
\leq
. The
layer
of an element
a\in A
is the size of the largest chain of
\leq
which includes
a
but no elements smaller than
a
, and a maximal element. For instance, the layer of a maximal element is
1
(the aforementioned largest chain includes just the element itself), the level of a strict predecessor of a maximal element is
2
, and so on. A subset
B\subseteq A
is called
k
-maximally layered
w.r.t.
\leq
if
B
is the set of all elements in
A
of layer
k^{\prime}\leq k
. When
\leq
is also a total order, the notion of maximally layered is equivalent to being upward closed w.r.t.
\leq
. Otherwise, it is equivalent to being upward closed w.r.t. every total extension of
\leq
.

A read specification
\mathsf{rspec}
is
k
-maximally layered
w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
) if for every object
x
, context
c=\{E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}\}
, and event
e
, either
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)=\emptyset
or
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)
is
k
-maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
). The
layer bound
of
\mathsf{rspec}
is defined as
k
. To cover cases where there is no such
k
, we say that a read specification
\mathsf{rspec}
is
\infty
-maximally layered
if for every
x
, context
c=\{E,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}\}
, and event
e
, either
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(x,c)=\emptyset
or
E
; and we say that the layer bound is
\infty
. When the layer bound and the partial order (
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
) are irrelevant, we simply say that
\mathsf{rspec}
is
maximally layered
.

Example 7.10.

For example,
\mathsf{faacas}
is
1
-maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
,
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
is
1
-maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
and
\mathsf{counter}
is
\infty
-maximally layered.
On the other hand, the read specification in
Example

7.9
is not maximally layered since it can sometimes return
init
from a non-empty context.

Lemma 7.11.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}
be a maximall-layered operation specification and let
\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
be a pair of consistency models such that
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
is stronger than
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
.
Any abstract execution valid w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{2},\mathsf{OpSpec})
is also valid w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{OpSpec})
.

Operation Closure.

As in
Section

4.2
, we assume that
\mathsf{OpSpec}
contains at least a read and a write event. Also, we assume that all objects support a common set of operations with identical read and write behavior, and that these operations can be executed at any replica. Formally, for every event
e\in E
, replica
\mathsf{r}
, and identifier
\mathsf{id}
, there exists an event
e^{\prime}
s.t.

\mathtt{rep}(e^{\prime})=\mathsf{r}
,
\mathtt{id}(e^{\prime})=\mathsf{id}
,
\mathsf{obj}({e^{\prime}})=\mathsf{obj}({e})
,
\mathsf{rspec}(e^{\prime})=\mathsf{rspec}(e)
,
\mathsf{extract}(e^{\prime})=\mathsf{extract}(e)
, and
\mathsf{wspec}(e^{\prime})=\mathsf{wspec}(e)
.

We also assume that operations apply uniformly to any set of objects. To formalize this assumption, we define a notion of
domain
for an operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
which is any set of objects
D
s.t. there is an event
e\in\mathsf{Events}[{\mathsf{OpSpec}}]
for which
\mathsf{obj}({e})=D
. We assume that domains are “symmetric”, i.e. if
D
is a domain for
\mathsf{OpSpec}
, then for every pair of objects
x\in D
and
y\in\mathsf{Objs}\setminus D
, the set
D^{\prime}=D\setminus\{x\}\cup\{y\}
is also a domain for
\mathsf{OpSpec}
. If
\mathsf{OpSpec}
allows single-object read/write/read-write events (defined as in
Section

4.2
), we assume that for every object
x
, there exists a read/write/read-write event whose domain is
\{x\}
. Also, we assume that if
\mathsf{OpSpec}
allows a
multi-object
read/write/read-write event
e
such that
\mathsf{obj}({e})
is a finite set of size at least
2
, then for every non-empty finite set
D\subseteq\mathsf{Objs}
,
D
is a domain of a read/write/read-write event in
\mathsf{OpSpec}
.

Correctors.

In addition, we assume that if
\mathsf{OpSpec}
permits conditional read-write events–which write to a set of objects
X
based on values they read (possibly from other objects) in some context–then any execution can be extended with some conditional read-write event
e
that writes to every object in
X
, modulo a so-called correction defined below. This property is only relevant for events with
|\mathsf{obj}({e})|>1
(and therefore, irrelevant for basic storage specifications). Our proof will rely on the existence of such extensions.

Example 7.12.

To provide some intuition about the need for corrections, consider a specification formed of prefix consistency (
PC
) and an operation specification with two multi-object operations,
\mathtt{InsAbs}
and
\mathtt{DelPre}
, under Last-Writer-Wins (LWW) conflict resolution (i.e., the read specification selects the maximal invocation from the context w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
) (see
Section

A.4
).
\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,v)
checks for every object
x\in X
if it is present, and inserts it with value
v
if not, and
\mathtt{DelPre}(X)
deletes every object
x\in X
as long as it was present.Assume an abstract execution
\xi
, and an event
e
from
\xi
whose context implies that
x
is absent and
y
is present. If
e
is an invocation of
\mathtt{InsAbs}
(resp.,
\mathtt{DelPre}
), then it can not write both objects since
x
is absent and
y
is present.

We introduce the notion of
corrector
, a set of auxiliary events that modify the context, ensuring the existence of an event that can write to both objects. For instance, in the scenario presented in
Example

7.12
, if
e
is an invocation of
\mathtt{InsAbs}(\{x,y\},1)
, the corrector will add a
\mathtt{DelPre}(\{y\})
invocation in its context, so both objects are absent.

We start by defining some notations. Let
\mathsf{Spec}=(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
be a storage specification,
\xi={({h,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}
an abstract execution of a history
h={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
, and
e\in E
an event. A
correction
of
e
in
\xi
with an event
a
, denoted by
\xi\overset{a}{\curlyvee}e
, is an abstract execution
\xi^{\prime}={({h^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}^{\prime}})}
associated to a history
h^{\prime}={({E\cup\{a\},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}^{\prime}})}
obtained by adding
a
as the immediate
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
-predecessor and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
-predecessor of
e
. If
\mathtt{rep}(e)=\mathtt{rep}(a)
, then
a
is also the immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-predecessor of
e
. The write-read dependencies (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}^{-1}
) of every event in
\xi
remain the same. Multiple corrections exist because the write-read and receive-before dependencies of
a
are not constrained. This allows flexibility on correcting
\xi
while preserving validity w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
.

The correction of
\xi
with a sequence of events
\vec{\mathsf{s}}=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
, denoted by
\xi\overset{\vec{\mathsf{s}}}{\curlyvee}e
, is defined as expected, by iteratively correcting
\xi
with all events in
\vec{\mathsf{s}}
in the order defined by
\vec{\mathsf{s}}
. Therefore, if
e^{\prime}
is the immediate
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
-predecessor of
e
in
\xi
, the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
order in
\xi\overset{\vec{\mathsf{s}}}{\curlyvee}e
will have
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
inserted in between
e^{\prime}
and
e
(in this order). Similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
and possibly for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
.

For a (partial) mapping
f:A\to B
and a total order
<
over
A
, the sequence of elements in
B
mapped by
f
and ordered according to
<
is denoted by
\mathsf{seq}_{<}(f)
. Formally,
\mathsf{seq}_{<}(f)=(f(a_{1}),f(a_{2}),\ldots)
such that
f(a_{i})\downarrow
and
a_{i}<a_{i+1}
for all
i
. We omit the subscript
<
when it is understood from the context.

Also, if
\xi
is an abstract execution, then
\xi\oplus e
is an abstract execution obtained from
\xi
by appending
e
to
\xi
as the last event w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
.

Corrector Assumption.

If
\mathsf{OpSpec}
allows conditional read-writes, then we assume that for every domain
D
,
W\subseteq D
,
x\in\mathsf{Objs}
s.t.
x\in W
if
W\neq\emptyset
, and abstract execution
\xi
, there exists

(1)

a conditional read-write
e
with
\mathsf{obj}({e})=D
which is not contained in
\xi
, and

(2)

a partial mapping
a:D\setminus\{x\}\to\mathsf{Events}
called
execution-corrector
for event
e
in an abstract execution
\xi\oplus e
.

We define execution-correctors as follows.

Definition 7.13.

Let
\mathsf{Spec}=(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
be a storage specification,
\xi
an abstract execution,

e
a conditional read-write event from
\xi

with
\mathsf{obj}({e})=D
,
W\subseteq D
a set of objects, and
x\in D
an object s.t.
x\in W
if
W\neq\emptyset
. Also, let
<
be a fixed total order on the set of objects. An
execution-corrector
for
{({e,W,x,\xi})}
is a partial mapping
a:D\setminus\{x\}\to\mathsf{Events}
such that if

\displaystyle\ \xi^{\prime}=\xi\overset{\mathsf{seq}(a)}{\curlyvee}e\text{ and }\xi^{\prime}\restriction y=(\xi\overset{\mathsf{seq}(a\restriction y)}{\curlyvee}e)\setminus\{e\}\text{ where }a\restriction y=a\restriction_{\{z\in\mathsf{Dom}(a)\ |\ z\leq y\}},

then the following hold:

(1)

for every
y\in D\setminus\{x\}
, if
a(y)
is defined and the correction up to
a(y)
is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
, then
a(y)
writes only
y
in the correction: if
a(y)\downarrow
and
\xi^{\prime}\restriction y
is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
, then for every object
z\in\mathsf{Objs}
,
\mathsf{wspec}(a(y))(z,[\xi^{\prime}\restriction y,\mathsf{CMod}])\downarrow
iff
z=y
, and

(2)

for every
y\in D
, if the correction is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
, then
e
reads
y
and additionally,
e
writes
y
only if
y\in W
, i.e.,
\mathsf{rspec}(e)(y,[\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{CMod}])\neq\emptyset
and
\mathsf{wspec}(e)(y,[\xi^{\prime},\mathsf{CMod}])\downarrow
iff
y\in W
.

Example 7.14.

We illustrate execution-correctors for the storage specification presented in
Example

7.12
, with
\mathtt{InsAbs}
and
\mathtt{DelPre}
as operations and
PC
as consistency model.

Let
\xi
be an abstract execution,
e
a
\mathtt{DelPre}(D)
event from
\xi
,
W\subseteq D
a non-empty set of objects and
x\in W
. For every object
y
, let
w_{y}
be the last event from the “read” context of
e
w.r.t.
PC
which writes
y
(by read context we mean the set of events selected by
\mathsf{rspec}
from the context). In the following we assume that
w_{x}
is an insert event. Note that if
w_{x}
is a delete event, then there exists no execution-corrector for
e
(intuitively, the correction concerns objects different from
x
, and
\mathtt{DelPre}(D)
will not delete an object which is already deleted).

An execution-corrector for
(e,W,x,\xi)
is the mapping
a:D\setminus\{x\}\to\mathsf{Events}
defined below. The mapping
a
observes the update on
y
made by
w_{y}
, and overwrites it when necessary. Thus, when
e
reads
y
,
y
is present iff
y\in W
.

(21)

a(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathtt{InsAbs}(\{y\},v)&\text{if }y\in W\text{ and }w_{y}\text{ deletes }y\text{ in }\xi\\
\mathtt{DelPre}(\{y\})&\text{if }y\not\in W\text{ and }w_{y}\text{ inserts }y\text{ in }\xi\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Observe that requiring that
a
is defined for all objects in
D
is too strict: if the read specification has a layer-bound of
1
and the events read a single object (as
\mathsf{faacas}
), any correction will change the entire context read by
e
.

8.
The Arbitration-Free Consistency Theorem

We now present our main result in its most general form, which extends Theorem
6.3
.

Theorem 8.1 (Arbitration-Free Consistency (AFC)).

Let
\mathsf{Spec}=(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
be a storage specification. The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\mathsf{CMod}
is arbitration-free w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
,

(2)

there exists an available
\mathsf{OpSpec}
-implementation.

The proof of (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) is very similar to that in
Theorem

6.3
(see
Section

6.1
). The only difference is replacing
Lemma

6.5
with
Lemma

7.11
where we use the maximally-layered assumption of read specifications. For the reverse, we follow the reasoning explained in the beginning of
Section

6.2
to reduce to consistency models in normal form.
Lemma

8.2
extends the arguments in
Lemma

6.7
to generalized storage specifications.

Lemma 8.2.

Let
\mathsf{Spec}=(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
be a storage specification. Assume that
\mathsf{CMod}
contains a simple visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
which is non-vacuous w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
, such that for some
i,0\leq i\leq\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})
,
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}={\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
. Then, there is no available
(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
-implementation.

Proof Sketch.

As in
Lemma

6.7
, we assume by contradiction that there is an available implementation
I_{E}
of
\mathsf{Spec}
. We use the visibility formula
\mathsf{v}
to construct a specific program, which by the assumption, admits a trace (in the composition with this implementation) that contains no
\mathtt{receive}
action. We show that any abstract execution induced by this trace, which is admissible by any available implementation of
\mathsf{Spec}
, is not valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
. This contradicts the hypothesis.

Let
d_{\mathsf{v}}
be the largest index
i
s.t.
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}={\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
(last occurrence of
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
). Then,
\mathsf{v}
is formed of two parts: the path of dependencies from
\varepsilon_{0}
to
\varepsilon_{d_{\mathsf{v}}}
which is not arbitration-free, and the suffix from
\varepsilon_{d_{\mathsf{v}}}
up to
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}
, the arbitration-free part.

The program
P
that we construct uses two replicas
r_{0},r_{1}
, two objects
x_{0},x_{1}
and a collection of events
e_{i}^{x_{l}},0\leq i\leq\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v}),l\in\{0,1\}
. The events are used to “encode” two instances of
\mathsf{v}_{x_{0}}
and
\mathsf{v}_{x_{1}}
. Replica
r_{l}
executes first events
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
with
i<d_{\mathsf{v}}
and then, events
e_{i}^{x_{1-l}}
with
i\geq d_{\mathsf{v}}
– the replica
r_{l}
executes the non arbitration-free part of
\mathsf{v}
for object
x_{l}
and the arbitration-free suffix of
\mathsf{v}
for
x_{1-l}
. For every
l
, the event
e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{l}}
reads
x_{1-l}
.

For ensuring that
\mathsf{v}_{x}(e_{0}^{x_{l}},\ldots e_{n}^{x_{l}})
holds in an induced abstract execution of a trace without
\mathtt{receive}
actions, we require that if
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, then
e_{i-1}^{x_{l}}
is a write event and
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
is a read event. For ensuring that
\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(e_{0},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})})
holds in such an abstract execution, for each set
E\in\mathcal{P}(\varepsilon_{0},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})})
s.t.
\mathsf{conflict}(E)
occurs in
\mathsf{v}
, we consider a distinct object
y_{E}
, which is also distinct from
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
. These objects represent each conflict in
\mathsf{v}
in a distinct manner. Then, we require that events
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
write to object
y_{E}
iff
\varepsilon_{i}\in E
and to object
x_{l}
iff
\varepsilon_{i}
belongs to the set
E_{x}
s.t.
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(E_{x})
occurs in
\mathsf{v}
(since
\mathsf{v}
is conflict-maximal, there is only one occurrence of a
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}
predicate). In the case
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
is a conditional read-write, we add a set of events
A_{i}^{x_{l}}
that form an execution-corrector so
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(e_{0}^{x_{l}},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{l}})
holds in an abstract execution of a trace without
\mathtt{receive}
actions. These additional events do not write on objects
x_{0}
or
x_{1}
.

Figure

9
exhibits a diagram of the abstract execution of the program.

Abstract execution of a trace without
\mathtt{receive}

\mathsf{v}
.
\mathsf{A}_{i}^{x_{l}}
represents a sequence of events
a_{i}^{x_{l}}(y),y\in\mathsf{obj}({e_{i}^{x_{l}}})
associated to an execution-corrector. The auxiliary events in
\mathsf{A}_{i}^{x_{l}}
allow that, if
(e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{0}},e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{1}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
,
\mathsf{wrCons}^{\mathsf{v}}_{x}(e_{0}^{x_{0}},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})
holds, and thus
v_{x_{0}}(e_{0}^{x_{0}},e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})
holds as well.

The rest of the proof, which proceeds as follows, is a generalization of the proof of
Lemma

6.7
which takes into considerations the assumptions we make about storage specifications:

(1)

that contains no receive action (
Lemma

C.5
).

(2)

The trace
t
induces a history
h_{\mathsf{v}}={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
and an abstract execution
\xi_{\mathsf{v}}={({h,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
. As
I_{E}
is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
,
\xi_{\mathsf{v}}
is valid w.r.t.
\mathsf{Spec}
. Next, we prove that since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
, events in
\xi_{\mathsf{v}}
read the latests value w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
for any object. In particular, we deduce that
\xi_{v}
is valid w.r.t.
(\textup{{CC}},\mathsf{OpSpec})
(
Corollary

D.5
).

(3)

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
is a total order, either
(e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{0}},e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{1}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
or
(e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{1}},e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{0}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
. W.l.o.g., assume that
(e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{0}},e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{1}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
. By
Proposition

D.6
, we deduce that
e_{0}^{x_{0}}\in\mathsf{ctxt}_{x_{0}}(e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}},[\xi_{\mathsf{v}},\mathsf{CMod}])
. The proof is explained in
Figure

9
: if
(e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{0}},e_{d_{\mathsf{v}}-1}^{x_{1}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, then all events
e_{i}^{x_{0}}
form a path in such way that
\mathsf{v}_{x_{0}}(e_{0}^{x_{0}},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})
holds in
\xi_{\mathsf{v}}
. If some event
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
is a conditional read-write event, the predicate
\mathsf{conflict}_{x}(e_{0}^{x_{0}},\ldots e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})
holds in
\xi_{\mathsf{v}}
thanks to the corrector events
A_{i}^{x_{l}}
.

(4)

As
e_{0}^{x_{0}}\in\mathsf{ctxt}_{x_{0}}(e_{\mathsf{len}(v)}^{x_{0}},[\xi_{\mathsf{v}},\mathsf{CMod}])
but
(e_{0}^{x_{0}},e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})\not\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
(no message is received), we deduce in
Proposition

B.16
that
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
. By contrapositive, if
\mathsf{OpSpec}
would be layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
, as
e_{0}^{x_{0}}\in\mathsf{ctxt}_{x_{0}}(e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}},[\xi_{\mathsf{v}},\mathsf{CMod}])
, there would exist an event
e
s.t.
(e_{0}^{x_{0}},e)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}
and
e\in\mathsf{rspec}(e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})(x_{0},[\xi_{\mathsf{v}},\mathsf{CMod}])
. However, as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
,
\mathtt{rep}(e_{0}^{x_{0}})=\mathtt{rep}(e)=\mathtt{rep}(e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})
which is false because
\mathtt{rep}(e_{0}^{x_{0}})=r_{0}
and
\mathtt{rep}(e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}})=r_{1}
.

(5)

Since
\mathsf{rspec}
is maximally layered, we can show that the layer bound of
\mathsf{rspec}
is smaller than or equal to the number of arbitration-free suffixes of
\mathsf{v}
(
Proposition

B.17
). Observe that an event writes
x_{0}
only if it is
init
or is an event
e_{i}^{x_{l}}
s.t.
\varepsilon_{i}\in E_{x}
and
l=0
. Any such index
i
corresponds to a suffix of
\mathsf{v}
. By causal suffix closure, for any arbitration-free suffix
v^{\prime}
of
v
there is a visibility formula that subsumes
v^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{n}\mathsf{CMod}_{\mathsf{OpSpec}}
. As
d_{\mathsf{v}}
is the maximum index for which
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}={\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, the number of events writing
x_{0}
in replica
r_{1}
distinct from
init
coincide with the number of arbitration-free suffixes of
\mathsf{v}
. Hence, as
\mathsf{rspec}
is layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, if its layer bound would be greater than the number of arbitration-free suffixes,
e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}}
would necessarily read
x_{0}
from
init
(other events writing
x_{0}
are in replica
r_{0}
and
e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}
only reads from events in
r_{1}
). However, as
\mathsf{rspec}
is maximally-layered and
e_{0}^{x_{0}}
succeeds
init
w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
, we would conclude that
e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}}
would also read
x_{0}
from
e_{0}^{x_{0}}
. However, this is impossible as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
but
e_{0}^{x_{0}}
is in replica
r_{0}
and
e_{\mathsf{len}(\mathsf{v})}^{x_{0}}
is in replica
r_{1}
.

(6)

Lastly, we show in
Proposition

B.18
that if the layer bound of
\mathsf{rspec}
is smaller than or equal to the number of arbitration-free suffixes of
v
, then
v
is vacuous w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
, which contradicts the fact that
\mathsf{v}
is a visibility formula from the normal form
\mathsf{n}\mathsf{CMod}_{\mathsf{OpSpec}}
.
∎

Corollary

8.3
is an immediate consequence of
Theorem

8.1
and
Lemma

6.4
.

Corollary 8.3.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}
be an operation specification.
The strongest consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
for which
(\mathsf{CMod},\mathsf{OpSpec})
admits an available implementation is
CC
.

9.
Related Work and Discussion

The CAP conjecture
(
Brewer 2000
)
claims that a distributed key-value store cannot be both consistent, available and tolerate partitions. The proof of the
CAP theorem

(
Gilbert and Lynch 2002
)
, uses a so-called
split brain
behavior, where two sets of replicas are isolated from each other, and a
get
(read) operation misses the result of an
earlier

set
(write) operation (which completes before the get starts). We remark that our proof in
section

2
actually extends the proof of the CAP theorem so it holds without the real-time requirement used in the original proof
(
Gilbert and Lynch 2002
)
.

As pointed by some critiques of the CAP theorem (e.g.,
(
Kleppmann 2015
)
), the proof equates consistency with atomicity of read / write variables. Moreover, network partitioning is a stand-in for end-to-end delays in geo-distributed systems. The PACELC (
if Partition then Availability or Consistency, Else Latency or Consistency
) theorem
(
Abadi 2012
)
(see
(
Golab 2018
)
) captures these observations; its proof extends results proved for
sequential consistency

(
Lipton and Sandberg 1988
;
Attiya and Welch 1994
)
. These results are also proved for read / write variables, capturing key-value stores. In the executions we construct, messages between replicas are delayed, in a way that corresponds to split brain behavior, and our emphasis is on constructing the right interaction sequences. We believe this behavior can be used to extend the AFC theorem so it talks about latency, rather than availability, for the same interaction sequences.

The CALM (
consistency as logical monotonicity
)
conjecture
(
Hellerstein 2010
)
relates monotonicity of queries to lack of coordination.
Informally, it states that a query has a coordination-free execution strategy if and only if it is monotonic.
In order to make this statement more concrete, it is necessary do define what coordination freedom means. In their proof of the CALM theorem, Ameloot et al.
(
Ameloot et al. 2013
)
equate coordination-freedom with the ability of clients to produce an output even when there is no communication between replicas. The proof relies on a split brain behavior, somewhat similar to the one used in the CAP theorem
(
Gilbert and Lynch 2002
)
. Extensions of this theorem
(
Ameloot et al. 2015
)
equip replicas with knowledge of the data distribution.
The CALM theorem is motivated, in part, by Bloom
(
Alvaro et al. 2011
)
, a programming language that encourages order-insensitive programming. The applications they present are to key-value stores and to a shopping cart, essentially, a counter.
Later work extends the CALM approach to a programming environment for composing small
lattices

(
Alvaro et al. 2011
)
,
and relates it to CRDTs
(
Laddad et al. 2022
)
.

One key challenge in deriving our result is considering abstract, generic consistency models, while prior work considers specific models. The other challenge is to allow their composition with abstract, generic shared objects, while prior work mostly consider key-value stores. On the possibility side, this is facilitated by the relating arbitration-freeness to causality; the necessity side relies on finding carefully-designed client interactions that “stress” dependencies between replicas.

Defining available implementations for causal consistency has been considered in several works
(
Bailis et al. 2012
;
Lloyd et al. 2011
;
Lloyd et al. 2013
;
Bailis et al. 2013
)
. The work of
Attiya et al. 2017

(
Attiya et al. 2017
)
and
Mahajan et al. 2011

(
Mahajan et al. 2011
)
show that, in the case of multi-value registers, consistency models stronger than causal consistency cannot support available implementations. In
(
Attiya et al. 2017
)
the condition is
observable causal consistency
(OCC) whereas in
(
Mahajan et al. 2011
)
the condition is
real-time causal consistency
(RTC). The definition of both OCC and RTC are specific to multi-value registers, and the impossibility result depends on several restrictions that we do not consider. Both papers make some (nontrivial, but different) assumptions about the implementations. Furthermore, both of them do not truly prove a tight result: while both
(
Attiya et al. 2017
;
Mahajan et al. 2011
)
prove the positive result for CC, in
(
Attiya et al. 2017
)
, the impossibility is proved for OCC, and in
(
Mahajan et al. 2011
)
it is for RTC (both stronger than CC). Besides handling a more general class of operations, the AFC theorem is a strengthening of their results, as it applies to causal consistency and is therefore tight.

Our specification framework builds on previous work
(
Burckhardt 2014
;
Biswas and Enea 2019
;
Burckhardt et al. 2014
;
Cerone et al. 2015
)
. Similarly to Burckhardt et al.
(
Burckhardt et al. 2014
;
Burckhardt 2014
)
, storage system specifications decouple consistency from the object semantics. We re-use the same ideas of defining consistency using visibility formulas, contexts, and an arbitration relation. Our object semantics is split into several semantical functions (
\mathsf{rspec}
,
\mathsf{extract}
, and
\mathsf{wspec}
) in order to be more general (modeling transactions), and be able to express “normal” constrains. The extension to transaction isolation levels is similar to
Cerone et al. 2015

(
Cerone et al. 2015
)
and
Biswas and Enea 2019

(
Biswas and Enea 2019
)
.

The works of
(
Biswas and Enea 2019
;
Bouajjani et al. 2025
)
study the complexity of checking consistency under different scenarios. There is no apparent relation between the complexity of checking consistency and the existence of available implementations: the AFC theorem shows that Read Committed admits available implementations but Sequential Consistency does not whereas
(
Bouajjani et al. 2025
)
shows that checking consistency of an SQL history under Read Committed (equivalent to Return-Value) or Sequential Consistency is NP-complete (inclusion in NP is trivial for any model).

Appendix A
Examples of Operation Specifications

We present here several well-know operation specifications.

A.1.
Key-Value Store with Fetch-And-Add and Compare-And-Swap Operations

The Key-Value Store with Fetch-And-Add and Compare-And-Swap (
\mathsf{faacas}
) is an operation specification with four operations,
\mathtt{PUT}(x,v)
, that puts value
v
to object
x
,
\mathtt{GET}(x)
that reads object
x
,
\mathtt{FAA}(x,v)
that reads the value
v^{\prime}
of object
x
and writes
v^{\prime}+v
, and
\mathtt{CAS}(x,v,v^{\prime})
, that reads
x
and writes
v^{\prime}
iff the value read is
v
.

The following equations, corresponding to
Equations

11
,
14
and
17
, describe the operation specification of
\mathsf{faacas}
.

(22)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}}E\}&\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{GET}(x),\mathtt{FAA}(x,v),\mathtt{CAS}(x,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\}\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(23)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{GET}(x),\mathtt{FAA}(x,v),\mathtt{CAS}(x,v,v^{\prime})\}\text{ and }R=\{(w,v)\}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(24)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{PUT}(x,v^{\prime})\\
v+v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{FAA}(x,v^{\prime})\\
v^{\prime\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{CAS}(x,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\land v=v^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The
\mathsf{faacas}
is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, with
1
as its layer bound. As
\mathtt{CAS}
is a single-object conditional read-write operation, it trivially allows execution-correctors.

A.2.
Key-Value Multi-Value Store

The Key-Value Multi-Value Store (
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
)
(
Burckhardt et al. 2014
;
Attiya et al. 2017
)

is an operation specification with two operations,
\mathtt{GET}(x)
, reading multiple concurrent values on a single object
x
, and
\mathtt{PUT}(x,v)
, writing on a single object
x
the value
v
.

The following equations, corresponding to
Equations

12
,
14
and
17
, describe the operation specification of
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
.

(25)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}}E\}&\text{if }r=\mathtt{GET}(x)\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(26)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{v\ |\ (\_,v)\in R\}&\text{if }r=\mathtt{GET}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(27)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,\_)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\text{if }w=\mathtt{PUT}(x,v)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{v}
is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
, with
1
as its layer bound.

A.3.
Distributed Counter

The distributed counter (
\mathsf{counter}
)
(
Burckhardt et al. 2014
)
is an operation specification with two operations,
\mathtt{inc}(x)
, incrementing the value of
x
by
1
, and
\mathtt{rd}(x)
, reading the amount of increments of
x
.

The following equations, corresponding to
Equations

13
,
16
and
19
, describe the operation specification of
\mathsf{counter}
.

(28)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}E&\text{if }r=\mathtt{rd}(x)\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(29)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}|R|-1&\text{if }r=\mathtt{rd}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(30)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,\_)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if }w=\mathtt{inc}(x)\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The
\mathsf{counter}
is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, with
\infty
as its layer bound.

A.4.
Insert/Delete Last-Write-Wins

The Insert/Delete Last-Write-Wins (
\mathsf{ins/del}
) is an operation specification with two multi-object operations.
\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,v)
checks for every object
x\in X
if it is present, and inserts it with value
v
if not, and
\mathtt{DelPre}(X)
deletes every object
x\in X
as long as it was already present.

Its operation specification is described as follows:

(31)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}}E\}&\text{if }r\in\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,v),\mathtt{DelPre}(X)\end{array}\right\},x\in X\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(32)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\text{if }w\in\{\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,\_),\mathtt{DelPre}(X)\},x\in X\text{ and }R=\{(\_,v)\}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\\
\right.

(33)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v^{\prime}&\text{if }w=\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,v^{\prime})\land v=\dagger\\
\dagger&\text{if }w=\mathtt{DelPre}(X)\land v\neq\dagger\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

where
\dagger
is a special value representing absence. We assume that
\mathtt{InsAbs}(X,\dagger)
is not defined.

The
\mathsf{ins/del}
is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, with
1
as its layer bound.
\mathsf{ins/del}
allows execution-correctors: let
\mathsf{CMod}
be a consistency model,
\xi
be an abstract execution,
D
be a domain,
W\subseteq D
be a set of objects and
x
be an object s.t.
x\in W
if
W\neq\emptyset
.

Let be
v
the value that event
e
reads in
\xi\oplus e
. If
v=\dagger
, we select
e=\mathtt{InsAbs}(D,\_)
while otherwise,
e=\mathtt{DelPre}(D)
. The mapping
a
below is an execution-corrector for
(e,W,x,\xi)
:

(34)

a(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathtt{InsAbs}(\{y\},v^{\prime})&\text{if }y\in W\land v_{y}=\dagger\neq v,\text{ or }y\not\in W\land v_{y}=\dagger=v\\
\mathtt{DelPre}(\{y\})&\text{if }y\in W\land v_{y}\neq\dagger=v,\text{ or }y\not\in W\land v_{y}\neq\dagger\neq v\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

where
v_{y}=\mathsf{wspec}(e_{p})(y,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])
and
e_{p}
is the maximal event w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
on the same replica as
e
.

A.5.
Non-Transactional SQL with Last-Writer-Wins Store

The Non-Transactional SQL with Last-Writer-Wins Store (
\mathsf{simple}
-
\mathsf{SQL}
) is an operation specification modelling SQL-like databases
(
Adya 1999
)
. Each object represents a row identifier and the set of values is defined abstractly as
\mathsf{Rows}
.
\mathsf{Rows}
contain a special value denoted
\dagger
, different from
\bot
, indicating that the row is deleted.

This operation specification employs four operations:
\mathtt{INSERT}
,
\mathtt{SELECT}
,
\mathtt{UPSERT}
and
\mathtt{DELETE}
. Each operation has a finite set of objects
D
as domain.
\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}})
inserts in the database each row
r
on an object
d\in D
using the mapping
\mathsf{R}:D\to\mathsf{Rows}
.
\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}})
selects the rows on the storage satisfying the predicate
\mathsf{p}:D\times\mathsf{Rows}\to\{\mathsf{false},\mathsf{true}\}
.
\mathtt{UPSERT}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}})
updates the rows that satisfy
\mathsf{p}
using the mapping
\mathsf{U}:D\times\mathsf{Rows}\to\mathsf{Rows}
, inserting them if they are absent. Finally,
\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})
, deletes the objects satisfying the predicate (i.e. replaces its row by
\dagger
). We assume that in for any predicate
\mathsf{p}
and object
x
,
\mathsf{p}(x,\dagger)=\mathsf{false}
.

(35)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}}E\}&\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}}),\mathtt{UPSERT}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}}),\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})\}\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(36)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if }r\in\{\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}}),\mathtt{UPSERT}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}}),\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})\},\\
\quad R=\{(w,v)\}\text{ and }\mathsf{p}_{x}(v)\end{array}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(37)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{R}(x)&\text{if }w=\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}})\\
\mathsf{U}_{x}(v)&\text{if }w=\mathtt{UPSERT}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}})\\
\dagger&\text{if }w=\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})\land v\not\in\{\bot,\dagger\}\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The
\mathsf{simple}
-
\mathsf{SQL}
is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
, with
1
as its layer bound.
\mathsf{simple}
-
\mathsf{SQL}
allows execution-correctors: let
\mathsf{CMod}
be a consistency model,
\xi
be an abstract execution,
D
be a domain,
W\subseteq D
be a set of objects and
x
be an object s.t.
x\in W
if
W\neq\emptyset
.

Let be
v
the value that event
e
reads in
\xi\oplus e
. We select the event
e=\mathtt{UPSERT}({\mathsf{p}_{D,W}},\,{\mathsf{U}_{D}})
, where
\mathsf{p}_{D,W}
and
\mathsf{U}_{D}
are defined below.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{p}_{D,W}(d,r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{true}&\text{if }d\in W\\
\mathsf{false}&\text{if }d\in D\setminus W\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\mathsf{U}_{D}(d,r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r&\text{if }d\in D\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\end{array}

For such event, we define the execution-corrector
a:D\setminus\{x\}\to\mathsf{Events}
as the totally-undefined mapping, i.e. the function that no object
y\in D
is associated with some event.

A.6.
Transactional SQL Multi-Value Store

The Transactional SQL Multi-Value Store (
\mathsf{SQL}
-
\mathsf{mvr}
) is an operation specification modelling SQL-like databases using
transactions
. Each object represents a row identifier and the set of values,
\mathsf{Rows}
, is defined as in
Section

A.5
.

Transactions are blocks of simple instructions that are executed sequentially. Transactions start its execution by selecting a
snapshot
of the database (i.e. a mapping associating each object a constant value) from which operations can read. Each instruction may execute a writing operation, but its effect it is only viewed internally. After their completion, the writing effects of the transaction can be seen by other transactions; giving the impression of atomicity.

We model the store with the aid of a unique operation,
\mathtt{TRANSACTION}(\mathsf{body})
that reads the snapshot of the database and then executes the instructions declared in
\mathtt{C}
.
\mathtt{C}
is defined as a sequence of five type of operations:
\mathtt{INSERT}
,
\mathtt{SELECT}
,
\mathtt{UPDATE}
and
\mathtt{DELETE}
. Each operation has a finite set of objects
D
as domain.
\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}})
inserts in the database each row
r
on an object
d\in D
using the mapping
\mathsf{R}:D\to\mathsf{Rows}
.
\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}})
selects the rows on the storage satisfying the predicate
\mathsf{p}:D\times\mathsf{Rows}\to\{\mathsf{false},\mathsf{true}\}
.
\mathtt{UPDATE}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}})
updates the rows that satisfy
\mathsf{p}
using the mapping
\mathsf{U}:D\times\mathsf{Rows}\to\mathsf{Rows}
. Finally,
\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})
, deletes the objects satisfying the predicate (i.e. replaces its row by
\dagger
).
\mathsf{abort}
represents states declared by the user where the transaction should not execute any more instructions and any declared write should be aborted.
We assume that in for any predicate
\mathsf{p}
and object
x
,
\mathsf{p}(x,\dagger)=\mathsf{false}
.

We model snapshots as mappings
\mathsf{Objs}\to\mathsf{Vals}
. Unlike in
Section

A.5
,
\mathsf{SQL}
-
\mathsf{mvr}
requires that local effects of SQL-like instructions are only seen internally, during the execution of the transaction. Such effects are modelled in
Equation

38
as a recursive function that simulates the transaction execution w.r.t. a concrete object. The function
\mathsf{exe}
executes one instruction at a time, and it stops whenever all instructions are executed, indicating that the execution was correct, or halting it midway in case some abortion occurred (modelled with the constant value
\bot
).

(38)

\mathsf{exe}_{x}(\mathsf{body},\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{exe}_{x}(\mathsf{body}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})&\text{if }\mathsf{body}=e;\mathsf{body}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{exI}_{x}(e,\sigma)\text{ and }\sigma^{\prime}\neq(\bot,\mathsf{false})\\
\sigma&\text{if }\mathsf{body}=\emptyset\\
(\bot,\mathsf{false})&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

The behavior of each instruction is modelled in
Equation

39
, updating the snapshot in object
x
in a similar way as
\mathsf{wspec}
does in
Section

A.5
, and indicating if the event
e
indeed wrote object
x
.

(39)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathsf{exI}_{x}(e,(\sigma,\mathsf{w}))&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\sigma,\mathsf{w})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}})\\
(\sigma,\mathsf{w})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})\land\lnot\mathsf{p}_{x}(\sigma)\\
(\dagger,\mathsf{true})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{DELETE}({\mathsf{p}})\land\mathsf{p}_{x}(\sigma)\\
(\sigma,\mathsf{w})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{UPDATE}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}})\text{ and either }\lnot\mathsf{p}_{x}(\sigma)\text{ or }U_{x}(\sigma)\uparrow\\
(\mathsf{U}_{x}(\sigma),\mathsf{true})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{UPDATE}({\mathsf{p}},\,{\mathsf{U}}),\mathsf{p}_{x}(\sigma)\land U_{x}(\sigma)\uparrow\\
(\sigma,\mathsf{w})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}})\land\mathsf{R}(x)\uparrow\\
(\mathsf{R}(x),\mathsf{true})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}})\land\mathsf{R}(x)\downarrow\\
(\bot,\mathsf{false})&\text{if }e=\mathtt{abort}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

The operation specifications of
\mathsf{SQL}
-
\mathsf{mvr}
are an adaptation of those of
\mathsf{k}
-
\mathsf{mv}
:

(40)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}}E\}&\text{if }r=\mathtt{TRANSACTION}(\mathsf{body})\text{ and }c={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}})}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(41)

\mathsf{extract}(r)(x,R)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma^{\prime}&\text{if }r=\mathtt{TRANSACTION}(\mathsf{body}),\sigma=\{(v,\mathsf{false})\ |\ (w,v)\in R\}\\
&\quad\text{and }\sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{exe}_{x}(\mathsf{body},\sigma)\end{array}\right.\\

(42)

\mathsf{wspec}(w)(x,\sigma)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v&\text{if }r=\mathtt{TRANSACTION}(\mathsf{body}),\text{ and }\sigma=(v,\mathsf{true})\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\

The
\mathsf{SQL}
-
\mathsf{mvr}
operation specification is maximally layered w.r.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}^{+}
, with
1
as its layer bound.
\mathsf{SQL}
-
\mathsf{mvr}
allows execution-correctors: let
\mathsf{CMod}
be a consistency model,
\xi
be an abstract execution,
D
be a domain,
W\subseteq D
be a set of objects and
x
be an object s.t.
x\in W
if
W\neq\emptyset
.

We define
e=\mathtt{TRANSACTION}(\mathtt{SELECT}({\mathsf{p}_{D}});\mathtt{INSERT}({\mathsf{R}_{W}}))
, where
\mathsf{p}_{W}
and
\mathsf{U}_{D}
are defined below.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{p}_{D}(d,r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{true}&\text{if }d\in D\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\mathsf{R}_{W}(d)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\_&\text{if }d\in D\\
\mathsf{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\end{array}

where
\_
indicates some arbitrary unspecified value.

For such event, we define the execution-corrector
a:D\setminus\{x\}\to\mathsf{Events}
as the totally-undefined mapping, i.e. the function that no object
y\in D
is associated with some event.

Appendix B
Normal Form of a Consistency Model w.r.t. an Operation Specification

In this section, we prove the existence of a consistency model in normal form equivalent to a given one (
Theorem

B.1
), and we show as well that arbitration-freeness is well-defined (
Theorem

B.9
), i.e. that either all its normal forms are arbitration-free or none.

For compare consistency models when restricted to an operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
, we introduce the notion of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
-equivalence. Two consistency models
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
,
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
are
\mathsf{OpSpec}
-equivalent
, denoted
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}\equiv_{\mathsf{OpSpec}}\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
, if for every abstract execution of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
,
\xi
,
\xi
is valid w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{OpSpec})
iff
\xi
is valid w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{2},\mathsf{OpSpec})
. In particular, if
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
and
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
are equivalent, they are also
\mathsf{OpSpec}
-equivalent.
The converse is not true: vacuous visibility formulas under an operation specification
\mathsf{OpSpec}
may not be vacuous for every possible operation specification.

B.1.
Existence of a Normal Form of a Consistency Model

Theorem

B.1
states the existence of a normal form of a consistency model w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
.

Theorem B.1.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}
be an operation specification. For every consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
, there exists a consistency model that is in normal form w.r.t.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
and that is
\mathsf{OpSpec}
-equivalent to
\mathsf{CMod}
.

The proof of such result is divided in three parts, proving the existence of a consistency model with only simple visibility formulas (
Lemma

B.4
), proving that such model can be refined for removing vacuous visibility formulas (
Lemma

B.7
) and finally, showing that conflict-maximality can be assumed without loss of generality (
Lemma

B.8
).

Monotonicity

Maximally-layered operation specifications are
monotonic
. Intuitively, an operation specification is
monotonic
if (1) the values that are not read under a consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
should be also not read under a stronger model
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
, and (2) whenever some values are read under a consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
but not under a stronger one
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
, some other values must be read under
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
which were not visible under
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
.

Definition B.2.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}=(E,\mathsf{rspec},\mathsf{extract},\mathsf{wspec})
be an operation specification.
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is called
monotonic
if for every pair of consistency models
\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
,
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
, abstract execution
\xi
, event
r\in\xi
, and object
x
the following hold:

(1)

\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\subseteq\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\cup(\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\setminus\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}]))
.

(2)

if
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\setminus\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\setminus\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\neq\emptyset

Lemma B.3.

A maximally-layered operation specification is monotonic.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{OpSpec}
be a maximally-layered operation specification,
\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
be two consistency models s.t.
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
,
\xi
be an abstract execution,
r
be an event in
\xi
and
x
be an object. Observe that by the unconditional read property of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
(Property
2
of
Definition

7.4
), we can assume w.l.o.g. that
r
is a read event.

On one hand, we observe that if the layer bound of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is
\infty
,
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is trivially monotonic: as
r
is a read event and the layer bound of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is
\infty
,
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])=\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
and
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])=\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
. Using the fact that
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
, is easy to see that Properties
1
and
2
hold in this case.

On the other hand, if the layer bound of
\mathsf{OpSpec}
,
k
, is finite, let
\mathsf{R}
be the relation for which
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is
k
-maximally layered. For proving Property
1
of
Definition

B.2
, let us partition
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
in the three disjoint sets
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
described in
Equation

43
.

(43)

\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{1}\coloneqq&\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\\
C_{2}\coloneqq&\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\setminus\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\\
C_{3}\coloneqq&\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\setminus\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\\
\end{array}

We note that by Property
1
of
Definition

7.4
,
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
. As
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
, we deduce that
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
; so
\{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}\}
is indeed a partition of
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
. Observe that showing Property
1
of
Definition

B.2
is equivalent to show that
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\subseteq C_{1}\cup C_{3}
. By Property
1
of
Definition

7.4
,
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])=C_{1}\cup C_{2}\cup C_{3}
. We conclude the result by showing that
C_{2}\cap\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])=\emptyset
.

For showing it, we observe that the layer of an event
w
in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
is less or equal than the layer of
w
in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
: as
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
, every chain of events in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
containing
w
and ordered w.r.t.
\mathsf{R}
belongs to
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
. Thus, as
\mathsf{OpSpec}
is maximally layered, an event
w
in
C_{2}
does not belong to
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
: if
w\in C_{2}
, its layer in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
is greater than
k
; so it is also greater than
k
in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
. Hence, as
\mathsf{OpSpec}
has
k
as layer bound,
w\not\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
.

For proving Property
2
, we observe that if there exists an event
w\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\setminus\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
, then the layer of
w
in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
is greater than
k
. Let
k^{\prime}
be the layer of
w
and let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}
be a chain of
\mathsf{R}
of length
k^{\prime}
s.t.
e_{k^{\prime}}=w
. As the layer of
w
in
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
is
k
and
\mathsf{R}
is a partial order, there exists an event
e_{i},1\leq i\leq k
s.t.
e_{i}\in C_{3}
. We observe that as the layer of
w
is
k
, the layer of event
e_{i}
is
i
. Hence, as
\mathsf{rspec}
is
k
-maximally layered, we conclude that
e_{i}\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\setminus\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
.
∎

Lemma

7.11
shows that for maximally-layered operation specifications, ensuring a strong consistency criteria is enough for ensuring a weaker one. The proof relies on the fact that maximally-layered operation specifications are monotonic (
Lemma

B.3
).

See
7.11

Proof.

Let
h={({E,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a history and let
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}
and
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
be two consistency models s.t.
\mathsf{CMod}_{1}\preccurlyeq\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
. Let also
\xi={({h,{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}})}
be an abstract execution that witness the validity of
h
w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{2},\mathsf{OpSpec})
.
To prove that
\xi
also witnesses
h
’s validity w.r.t.
(\mathsf{CMod}_{1},\mathsf{OpSpec})
, by
Definition

7.8
, it suffices to prove that for every event
r\in h
and object
x
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)=\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\subseteq\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
:
Let
w
be a write event in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)
. As
(w,r)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
,
w\in\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
. Moreover, as
\xi
witnesses
h
’s validity w.r.t.
\mathsf{CMod}_{2}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)=\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
. Hence, as
w\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])\cap\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
, by Property
1
of
Definition

B.2
,
w\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\supseteq\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
:
Let
w\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
s.t.
w\not\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
. By property
2
from
Definition

B.2
, there exists
w^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])
s.t.
w^{\prime}\not\in\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
. However, as
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\subseteq\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])
, this is impossible. Therefore,
\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{1}])\subseteq\mathsf{rspec}(r)(x,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}_{2}])={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)
. ∎

An immediate consequence of
Lemma

7.11
is the following result.

See
6.5

Simple Form

For proving
Theorem

B.1
, we first prove the existence of a consistency model in simple form (i.e. a consistency model with all its visibility formulas are simple) that is equivalent to
\mathsf{CMod}
.

Lemma B.4.

For any consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
, there exists a consistency model in simple form that is equivalent to
\mathsf{CMod}
.

Intuitively, the proof of
Lemma

B.4
is as follows: we first unfold union and transitive closure operators, and then trim
\mathtt{id}
and compositional operators to obtain a consistency model in simple form.
As an intermediate step, we define the consistency model obtained after unfolding union and transitive closure operators. Such consistency model is the
almost simple form
of
\mathsf{CMod}
,
\mathsf{almost}(\mathsf{CMod})
, and it is described as the union of the
almost simple form
of each of its visibility formulas, i.e.
\mathsf{almost}(\mathsf{CMod})=\bigcup_{v\in\mathsf{CMod}}\mathsf{almost}(v)
. A visibility formula
a
belongs to the almost simple form of a visibility formula
v
,
a\in\mathsf{almost}(v)
if (1)
\mathsf{len}(v)=\mathsf{len}(a)
and (2) for every
i,1\leq i\leq\mathsf{len}(v)
,
\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{a}\in\sigma(\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}})
; where
\sigma(\mathsf{Rel}i^{v})
is the set of relations described as follows:

(44)

\sigma(\mathsf{R})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{\mathsf{R}\}&\text{if }\mathsf{R}=\mathtt{id},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}}\text{ or }{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}\\
\sigma(\mathsf{S})\cup\sigma(\mathsf{T})&\text{if }\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{S}\cup\mathsf{T}\\
\sigma(\mathsf{S});\sigma(\mathsf{T})&\text{if }\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{S};\mathsf{T}\\
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}\land k\geq 1}\sigma(\mathsf{S})^{k}&\text{if }\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{S}^{+}\end{array}\right.

where the composition of two sets of relations
A,B
is defined as
A;B\Coloneqq\{a;b\ |\ a\in A,b\in B\}
.

We prove that
\mathsf{CMod}
and
\mathsf{almost}(\mathsf{CMod})
are equivalent.

Proposition B.5.

For any consistency model
\mathsf{CMod}
,
\mathsf{CMod}
and
\mathsf{almost}(\mathsf{CMod})
are equivalent.

Proof.

For proving the result, we show that for any abstract execution
\xi
, object
x
and event
r
,
\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{CMod}])=\mathsf{ctxt}_{x}(r,[\xi,\mathsf{almost}(\mathsf{CMod})])
. In particular, it suffices to prove that for every visibility formula
v\in\mathsf{CMod}
and event
w
,
v_{x}(w,r)
holds in
\xi
iff there exists a visibility formula
a\in\mathsf{almost}(v)
s.t.
a_{x}(w,r)
holds in
\xi
. Observe that for every
a\in\mathsf{almost}(v)
,
\mathsf{len}(v)=\mathsf{len}(a)
; so we reduce the proof to show that for every pair of events
e,e^{\prime}
,
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}}
iff there exists
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{Rel}_{i}^{\mathsf{v}})
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
.

In the following, we prove that for every relation
\mathsf{R}
over pair of events obtained by the grammar described in
Equation

3
, the following holds:
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}
iff there exists
\mathsf{R^{\prime}}\in\sigma(R)
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
. We show the result by induction on the depth of
\mathsf{R}
6
6

6

By depth of
\mathsf{R}
we mean the depth of the tree obtained by deriving
\mathsf{R}
using
Equation

3
.
. The base case, when the depth of
\mathsf{R}
is
0
, refers to the case
\mathsf{R}=\mathtt{id},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.1484,1}\mathsf{rb}},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{ar}}
. In such case, the result immediately holds by the definition of
\sigma(\mathsf{R})
.

Let us assume that for any relation of depth at most
n
the result holds, and let us prove that for relations of depth
n+1
. Three alternatives arise:

•

If
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{S}\cup\mathsf{T}
,
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}
if and only if
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{S}\cup\mathsf{T}
. By induction hypothesis on both
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{T}
,
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{S}\cup\mathsf{T}
iff there exists
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{S})\cup\sigma(\mathsf{T})
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
. Finally, by
Equation

44
, we conclude that there exists
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{S})\cup\sigma(\mathsf{T})
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
if and only if there exists
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{R})
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
.

•

If
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{S};\mathsf{T}
,
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{R}
if and only if
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{S};\mathsf{T}
. By the definition of composition,
(e,e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{S};\mathsf{T}
iff there exists
e^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{S}
and
(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}
. By induction hypothesis on both
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{T}
, there exists
e^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{S}
and
(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}
iff there exists
e^{\prime\prime}
and relations
\mathsf{S}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{S}),\mathsf{T}^{\prime}\in\sigma(\mathsf{T})

e^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
(e,e^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{S}^{\prime}
and
(e^{\prime\prime},e^{\prime})\in\mathsf{T}^{\prime}
. By the definition of
\sigma(S);\sigma(T)
, we observe that there exists
e^{\prime\prime}
and relations
